### 14.9. Evolving the core together with its reservoir

Initial compatibility does not establish persistence. The high-frequency
member of Section 14.8 has many fast carrier oscillations per binding
time, so a direct relativistic grid is costly. We instead derive its
leading classical envelope, retain the solved reservoir and excited parent,
and test the reduction against the unchanged full Einstein--Klein--Gordon
solver on a short, tractable family. The long result belongs to the reduced
equations and their stated domain.

For the real displacement $\chi=\phi-\phi_{\min}$, define

$$
\chi=\sqrt{2/\kappa}\,\zeta^2 A^{-3/2}
\operatorname{Re}[\psi(x,\tau)e^{-i\omega t}],\qquad
x=\frac{\omega\zeta R}{A},\qquad \tau=\omega\zeta^2t.
$$

The normalized parent scale factor starts at $A=1$. The complex amplitude
$\psi$ describes the same real field; it is a total amplitude, including
its homogeneous value

$$
b=\frac{\sqrt{\kappa/2}\,d}{\omega\zeta^2}
=.000612372435696,\qquad d=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}.
$$

For this member $\omega=10^9$ and $\zeta=.001$. The initial field is the
previously supplied tapered core plus its depleted ambient annulus.
The real scalar value matches the exact constrained initial slice.
The initial normal momentum matches only to leading order, since the
slow prefactor contributes an $O(H/\omega)$ carrier-quadrature correction.
The small initial gradient-mass difference is recorded rather than removed
by renormalizing the field.

Averaging the homogeneous carrier retains the parent's excitation,
vacuum and positive spatial curvature:

$$
H^2=\frac{\Lambda_v}{3}+\frac{\kappa E_b}{3A^3}-\frac{C}{A^2},\qquad
\dot H=\frac{C}{A^2}-\frac{\kappa E_b}{2A^3}.
$$

Here $\Lambda_v=9/32$, $E_b=3/8$ and $C=.0227765023676$. Excitation is
initially $4/7$ of the parent density; a pure de Sitter replacement would
lose a leading contribution. On the local, nearly flat spatial chart,
the classical envelope obeys

$$
i\psi_\tau=-\frac{\Delta_x\psi}{2A^2}+V\psi,\qquad
\Delta_xV=\frac{|\psi|^2-b^2}{2A}.
$$

The complete density contrast is retained, including its coherent cross
term. The homogeneous background is already inside $\psi$, so it is not
added a second time. The physical Newtonian potential is $\zeta^2V$.
This uses the established real-scalar envelope framework in the explicit
source normalization of this atlas; it introduces no additional field or
measurement-probability law.

One exact check clarifies why the homogeneous envelope can be constant.
On the averaged parent, a field proportional to $A^{-3/2}\cos(\omega t)$
satisfies

$$
(\partial_t^2+3H\partial_t+\omega^2)\chi_b
=\left(-\frac{3}{2}\dot H-\frac{9}{4}H^2\right)\chi_b
=\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{C}{A^2}-\Lambda_v\right)\chi_b.
$$

The dust terms cancel. The residual is small relative to the carrier
term, about $2\times10^{-19}$ for this member. This is an exact identity
on the averaged background, not proof that it equals the full oscillating
Einstein-scalar background at every instant.

### 14.10. Conserved transport, finite persistence and a relativistic check

The envelope's geometric excess mass is

$$
\mathfrak{M}_{\rm env}(R)=\frac{\zeta}{2\omega}
\int_0^{\omega\zeta R/A}x^2(|\psi|^2-b^2)dx.
$$

The factor of one-half follows from the Einstein mass normalization.
The initial envelope core mass differs from its exact GR value by
$-3.31923\times10^{-7}$ relatively. That difference is retained. Continuity
fixes the current; at a fixed physical radius the moving comoving cutoff
$x_*=X/A$ adds a necessary term:

$$
\frac{dN_*}{d\tau}
=-\frac{x_*^2}{A^2}\operatorname{Im}(\psi^*\psi_x)
-\frac{A'}{A}x_*^3(|\psi|^2-b^2).
$$

Independently integrating this current tests the changing enclosed mass.
The energy functional has kinetic and gravitational parts

$$
T=\frac{1}{2A^2}\int x^2|\psi_x|^2dx,\qquad
W=\frac{1}{2}\int x^2V(|\psi|^2-b^2)dx.
$$

With the symmetric Newtonian kernel and a time-independent outer field
value $\psi=b$, the reduced equations imply

$$
\frac{d(T+W)}{d\tau}=-\frac{A'}{A}(2T+W).
$$

Both terms contribute to expansion work. No absorber discards outgoing
field. The outer mass current is measured, and a larger domain checks its
influence on the core. These finite envelope-domain checks are not a new
relativistic causal-shielding theorem.

Eleven numerical cases evolve the retained high-frequency preparation to
slow time $600$, with three resolutions and separate time, outer-boundary
and no-gravity controls. The parent scale factor reaches $1.27443638723$.
Over the late interval $480\leq\tau\leq600$, mass inside the original fixed
physical core cutoff has ratio

$$
\frac{\langle\mathfrak{M}_{\rm core}\rangle}
{\mathfrak{M}_{\rm core}(0)}=1.00374088\pm.00001314.
$$

This is a small inward transfer, checked by the mass current. With
self-gravity removed, the same late ratio is $.000190975$. The core's
late mean physical half-mass radius is $2.23255821$ in the scaled length
$\omega\zeta R$, with numerical comparison range $.00319767$.
Its radius breathes: the peak-to-peak range grows from $.0674$ in the first
$120$ slow-time units to $.1399$ in the last $120$. Finite localization
therefore does not establish asymptotic stability.

![Finite localization of the supplied core and reservoir in the expanding parent](../docs/figures/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-evolution.png)

*Figure 23. The same supplied field disperses without self-gravity and
retains a localized core when self-gravity is included. Its physical size
breathes while the parent expands; the dashed size reference is simply
initial radius multiplied by the parent scale factor. Mass-current and
energy-work checks improve under refinement. The last panel compares
the reduced equations with full EKG over a separate short interval.*

The mass-current discrepancy falls from $5.13\times10^{-6}$ through
$1.26\times10^{-6}$ to $3.24\times10^{-7}$ of initial core mass. The
energy-work discrepancy falls from $1.75\times10^{-5}$ to
$1.04\times10^{-6}$ of initial energy. The
[compact calculation](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-evolution-v1.json)
records source hashes, finite comparisons and the small net outer transport.
The [derivation and domain](../docs/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-evolution.md)
keep numerical error distinct from approximation error.

For the independent short comparison, hold $\omega\zeta^2=12.8$ and evolve
to atlas time $.4$, or slow time $5.12$. The original full EKG functions
are unchanged:

| Core amplitude | Frequency | Full-EKG mass ratio | Envelope mass ratio | Difference |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| .08 | 2000 | 1.007402025 | 1.007139302 | .000262723 |
| .04 | 8000 | 1.007190375 | 1.007121981 | .0000683932 |

Halving the amplitude reduces the discrepancy by about $3.84$, consistent
with weak-field ordering. Refining the smaller full-EKG case changes its
ratio by $2.15\times10^{-8}$, far below that difference. Analytic free
Gaussian propagation, an independent Gaussian gravitational potential
and moving-cut continuity provide additional checks.

Using the earlier supplied reference $\Lambda_v=10^{-52}\,{\rm m}^{-2}$,
the long run spans $3.36$ billion years. Its fixed mass cutoff is $34.4$ kpc
and its late mean half-mass radius is $3.837\pm.006$ kpc. Those physical
values inherit the assumed ruler; their quoted ranges are numerical ranges
within the envelope model. No galaxy observations were fitted.

The short full-equation comparison and small expansion, curvature and
weak-field parameters do not supply a rigorous full-GR error bound at slow
time $600$. The supplied initial cloud, growing radial breathing and
nonspherical persistence remain physical questions. The completed connection
is from the conserved initial supply construction to finite radial
localization in its expanding parent, with a stated approximation and
independent transport checks.

### 14.11. Testing the breathing core beyond spherical symmetry

A changing radius does not by itself diagnose an instability. We now
linearize the actual evolving envelope of Section 14.10 and calculate its
radial and quadrupole response. The spherical field, reservoir and parent
expansion are replayed unchanged. A stationary eigenproblem would answer
a different question because this background is time dependent.

Perturbations of Schrodinger--Newton stationary states have prior analyses
by [Harrison, Moroz and Tod](https://arxiv.org/abs/math-ph/0208045).
[Zagorac et al.](https://arxiv.org/abs/2109.01920) investigate breathing,
quadrupolar deformation and time-dependent soliton perturbations. Their
stability or profile conclusions are not substituted for this trajectory.

Use real angular functions with angular mean square one. For an
axisymmetric quadrupole, $Y_{20}=\sqrt{5}P_2$. With real perturbation
parameter $\varepsilon$, write

$$
\psi=\psi_0+\varepsilon\eta_lY_{lm}+O(\varepsilon^2),\qquad
V=V_0+\varepsilon v_lY_{lm}+O(\varepsilon^2).
$$

The real-linear equations are

$$
i\partial_\tau\eta_l=
\left[-\frac{D_l}{2A^2}+V_0\right]\eta_l+v_l\psi_0,\qquad
D_lv_l=\frac{\operatorname{Re}(\psi_0^*\eta_l)}{A},
$$

$$
D_l=\partial_x^2+\frac{2}{x}\partial_x-\frac{l(l+1)}{x^2}.
$$

The potential source is real, so complex phase directions are included
explicitly as independent real initial directions. With
$d_l=2\operatorname{Re}(\psi_0^*\eta_l)$, regularity and the decaying exterior
potential give

$$
v_l(x)=-\frac{1}{2A(2l+1)}
\left[\frac{1}{x^{l+1}}\int_0^x s^{l+2}d_l(s)ds
+x^l\int_x^L s^{1-l}d_l(s)ds\right].
$$

Both integrals are retained. The finite source is zero outside $L$;
the potential has its free decaying tail rather than a zero-potential wall.
The $l=2$ density has zero angular mean, so its radial coefficient integral
is not a monopole mass change.

During the joint potential substep the base field rotates but its density
and the density variation remain fixed. The exact tangent flow is therefore

$$
\eta_l^{\rm new}=e^{-ihV_0}(\eta_l-ihv_l\psi_0).
$$

This is also the derivative of the nonlinear potential step. In
$q=x\eta$ variables, the kinetic operator is
$-\partial_x^2/(2A^2)+l(l+1)/(2A^2x^2)$. The radial channel uses the
same spectral kinetic step as the base. The quadrupole uses a unitary
centrifugal step, checked against an analytic solid-harmonic Gaussian.

The transported bilinear form

$$
S_{jk}=\operatorname{Im}\int q_j^*q_k\,dx
$$

is conserved. The kinetic and background-potential terms cancel by
self-adjointness; the remaining source terms cancel through symmetry of
the multipole Green kernel. This symplectic identity remains valid for a
time-dependent background. It checks the transport but does not imply
norm conservation or stability. A separate passive quadrupole, propagated
without its own potential response, supplies the unitary control.

### 14.12. A finite response theorem for every orientation in a declared span

Choose two compact radial profiles of widths $2$ and $4$ in the physical
scaled radius $Ax$, together with their phase partners. They taper to zero
between radii $15$ and $20$. Project the radial seeds to preserve initial
total mass to first order; quadrupoles have zero angular mean automatically.
Real Gram orthonormalization gives four initial directions.

Let $N_c$ be the initial core-field norm. For any real coefficient vector
$c$ of unit Euclidean length, a fractional field perturbation is

$$
\delta q(\tau)=\varepsilon N_c\sum_{j=1}^{4}c_jq_j(\tau).
$$

Define $G_{jk}=\operatorname{Re}\int q_j^*q_kdx$. The Rayleigh quotient
then gives the exact finite-span bound

$$
\frac{\|\delta q(\tau)\|_2}{\varepsilon N_c}
\leq\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(G(\tau))}.
$$

A second real Gram matrix uses the density variations inside the fixed
physical core cutoff. Normalizing by the current base excess-density norm
gives the fractional density response per fractional initial field
perturbation. This is not a density-to-density growth factor: some initial
phase directions have zero density variation. The density response of the
direction with greatest field-norm growth is recorded separately.

Spherical symmetry gives the same radial propagator for all five real
quadrupole components. Angular orthogonality gives the direct-sum Gram
matrix $I_5\otimes G$, with the same largest eigenvalue. The four-column
calculation therefore covers every coefficient combination in the
corresponding twenty-dimensional real quadrupole span. A new quadrupole
family is also seeded on the evolved state at slow time $480$.

The normalized core second-moment quadrupole is defined explicitly by

$$
\frac{\int R^2P_2\,\delta\rho\,d^3R}
{\int R^2\rho_{\rm excess}\,d^3R}
=\frac{\varepsilon N_c}{\sqrt{5}}
\frac{\sum_jc_j\int x^4d_jdx}{\int x^4d_0dx}.
$$

The factor follows from the angular average of $P_2Y_{20}$, which is
$1/\sqrt{5}$. Direct angular integration independently verifies this
moment definition. It is distinct from the coefficient multiplying $P_2$
and from a Cartesian trace-free tensor component. A core-truncated moment
is not inserted into an exterior potential formula; that calculation
uses the full multipole source.

These are finite response statements on a specified radial span. Numerical
maxima are reported on saved unit-slow-time events, not as rigorous bounds
between every pair of samples. The result does not cover additional radial
profiles, reservoir-supported seeds, higher angular multipoles, nonlinear
three-dimensional evolution or full-GR stability.

The [response calculation](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-perturbations-v1.json)
reproduces every retained spherical field hash. Its sampled extrema are:

| Perturbation family | Field-norm amplification | Density response per initial field perturbation |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Radial, seeded at 0 | $6.756\pm.162$ | $3.592\pm.033$ |
| Quadrupole, seeded at 0 | $1.08113\pm.00025$ | $1.15675\pm.00007$ |
| Quadrupole, seeded at 480 | $1.0820\pm.0011$ | $1.1720\pm.0049$ |

For example, a $1%$ fractional field-norm perturbation in the quadrupole
span seeded at time zero gives a calculated first-order density response
of at most about $1.157%$ at the saved events. The quoted ranges compare
spatial/time refinement and the outer domain. They are not rigorous
interval enclosures, bounds between samples, or nonlinear guarantees at
finite amplitude. Different observables can have different maximizing
initial coefficient vectors.

Radial sensitivity is larger and more dependent on refinement. At the final
event, the direction with greatest field amplification has $99.4%$ of
its squared norm inside the core; $99.5%$ of that core part lies in the
local phase direction relative to the base field. This does not make every
radial response a phase change. Spatial phase gradients can affect later
flow, and the independent density response is nonzero.

The symplectic discrepancy stays below $1.15\times10^{-11}$ in the radial
channel and $9.76\times10^{-14}$ in the quadrupole. The passive quadrupole
norm remains one within $5\times10^{-14}$. Independently, centred differences
of the nonlinear radial flow converge toward the tangent prediction:
halving the initial amplitude from $10^{-4}$ to $5\times10^{-5}$ reduces
the density-derivative error from $2.18\times10^{-6}$ to $5.46\times10^{-7}$.

![Radial sensitivity and quadrupole response on the retained evolving source](../docs/figures/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-perturbations.png)

*Figure 24. Field sensitivity and density deformation are distinct responses.
The quadrupole field response remains modest for seeds at the initial and
late events; the same radial matrices cover all five angular components
in the declared span. Independent nonlinear radial differences converge to
the tangent prediction. These finite sampled results do not establish
universal or asymptotic stability.*

### 14.13. The surrounding field must carry the tidal phase

A quadrupole has zero angular mean, so its first-order monopole mass change
vanishes. It can nevertheless produce a nonzero external tidal potential.
The full multipole kernel was therefore evaluated at fixed physical radius
$2R_d$, outside the initial reservoir. Cauchy--Schwarz bounds each response
coefficient. The squared angular basis functions sum to five at every direction, so
the pointwise all-orientation potential bound is $\sqrt{5}$ times its
angular-RMS bound.

The initial external amplitude failed its numerical comparison. At fixed
spacing, enlarging the domain changed the coefficient from
$3.33\times10^{-14}$ to $9.28\times10^{-15}$. This is a diagnostic value,
not an accepted nonzero tidal prediction. The failure identifies an outer
condition that matters for the observer calculation.

In an ideal homogeneous ambient region, with no incoming density disturbance,
let the quadrupolar potential be $v_2=C(\tau)/x^3$. It is harmonic:
$D_2v_2=0$. The linearized field equation has the particular solution

$$
\eta_2(x,\tau)=-i b x^{-3}\int C(s)ds,\qquad
2\operatorname{Re}(b\eta_2)=0.
$$

It carries a phase tail without a first-order density change. Therefore

$$
q=x\eta_2\propto x^{-2},\qquad q_x=-\frac{2q}{x}.
$$

A zero-field condition at a finite outer radius suppresses this forced
phase and can generate spurious density there. A separate calculation
allows the leading harmonic tail through a self-adjoint boundary correction.
Its Cayley step remains unitary and agrees with an independent dense-matrix
calculation. General outgoing radiative or Jeans responses are not replaced
by this particular harmonic condition.

The [tail-matching comparisons](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-tidal-tail-v1.json)
reduce the outer-domain change from $2.40\times10^{-14}$ to
$7.26\times10^{-17}$, an improvement of about $331$. The final core-density
response changes by less than $1.8\times10^{-8}$, supporting the core result
against this boundary change.

The external amplitude still fails the refinement requirement: its
coarse-to-medium change is $8.80\times10^{-15}$, so twice that change
exceeds the medium coefficient $9.34\times10^{-15}$. The public record
explicitly leaves this outward prediction unresolved. The useful results
are the finite core-response bound and the derived tidal-phase matching
requirement. An external clock or lens prediction needs further controlled
work; it is not inferred from the unresolved coefficient.

The [perturbation owner](../docs/nsc-atlas-hierarchy-perturbations.md)
records the definitions, interpretation, independent checks and compute
limits. The original response campaign used about $4073$ CPU seconds
within its $4500$-second budget. After the diagnosed boundary failure,
the targeted tail calculation used about $615$ within its separate
$800$-second budget. Both retain their completed evidence without enlarging
their claims to all-profile, nonlinear or full-relativistic stability.

### 14.14. A local concentration carries the signature of its surroundings

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-compensation-response -->

Derivation and calculation: [spatial compensation and retained evolution](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-compensation-response-v1.json)
and [derivation and source dictionary](../docs/nsc-atlas-compensation-response.md).

The dense core and its depleted surroundings are one initial solution.
Their relation should therefore survive into a spatial statistic. Let
$M(R)$ be excess geometric mass, $M_c$ its value at
the core cutoff, and $R_d$ the solved compensation radius. On the retained
zero-momentum initial slice,

$$
\frac{dM}{dR}=\frac{\kappa R^2}{2}
\left(U-U_0+\frac{BF^2}{2}\right),\qquad
M(0)=M(R_d)=0.
$$

This is the areal mass measure from the Einstein constraint. A proper-volume
energy integral is a different object. With $s=R/R_d$ and $K=kR_d$, define

$$
W(K)=\frac{1}{M_c}\int j_0(Ks)\,dM,
\qquad j_0(x)=\frac{\sin x}{x}.
$$

The transform is an exact descriptor of the specified mass profile. Its
flat-space density interpretation is local, on scales small compared with
the parent's curvature radius. It does not substitute continuous Fourier
modes for the compact parent's global spectrum. Compensation gives

$$
W(K)=a_2K^2+\mathcal R_4(K),\qquad
a_2=-\frac{1}{6M_c}\int s^2\,dM,
$$
$$
|\mathcal R_4(K)|\leq
\frac{K^4}{120M_c}\int s^4|dM|.
$$

To prove this bound, write $j_0(x)$ as the integral of $\cos(xt)$ from
zero to one and integrate the fourth-order cosine remainder. The zeroth
moment cancels, and spherical symmetry eliminates the dipole. The retained
profile gives $a_2=.0608058925116$ and remainder coefficient
$.00129586307998$. Integrating the solved mass primitive against $Kj_1(Ks)$
independently reproduces the transform without differentiating stored data.

The simpler initial scalar envelope omits positive gradient mass. Using
the same normalization in both descriptions yields an all-wavelength bound:

$$
dM-dM_{\rm env}
=\frac{\kappa R^2BF^2}{4}dR\geq0,\qquad
|W-W_{\rm env}|\leq g
=\frac{1}{M_c}\int\frac{\kappa R^2BF^2}{4}dR.
$$

For this source $g=3.31911631\times10^{-7}$. The initial envelope has
$W_{\rm env}(0)=-g$; its small residual is retained. Near $K=.00234$ it
competes with the quadratic term. Thus the absolute source bound cannot
be converted into arbitrary relative accuracy near zero. Nor is it a
long-time error bound for the reduced dynamics or an assertion that the
instantaneous scalar pressure is dustlike.

Let $W_c$ denote the core-only transform, with the same normalization.
The resolved half-power crossing is defined by

$$
\left[\frac{W(K_{1/2})}{W_c(K_{1/2})}\right]^2=\frac{1}{2}.
$$

For the unchanged $\omega=10^9$, $\zeta=.001$, half-depletion preparation,

$$
K_{1/2}=4.0522389138,\qquad
\frac{\Lambda_v}{M_c k_{1/2}^3}=.2681493031.
$$

The second number combines the already solved mass--reservoir--vacuum
relation with the new spatial window. Three quadrature orders and a
tighter constraint integration give comparison ranges below $9\times10^{-12}$
and $1.8\times10^{-12}$, respectively. Those ranges concern integration
of the fixed numerical core shape, not total model uncertainty.

At fixed weak amplitude and depletion, the established large-frequency
hierarchy makes the core pointlike relative to its reservoir. Its window
tends to one minus the transform of the normalized positive weight
$s^2[2ab(s)-a^2b(s)^2]/J(a)$. Half depletion gives limiting crossing
$4.1423205290$ and limiting vacuum--mass--turnover ratio $.2578964084$.
Finite quarter- and three-quarter-depletion preparations instead give
$.47250817$ and $.20537800$. Inheritance has not selected one depletion.

With the previously supplied $\Lambda_v=10^{-52}\,\mathrm{m}^{-2}$ ruler,
the initial example has $R_d=.827653$ Mpc, $k_{1/2}=4.89606$ Mpc$^{-1}$
and wavelength $2\pi/k_{1/2}=1.28331$ Mpc. These are conditional spatial
numbers, not an observed halo population or a fitted cosmological spectrum.

![Compensated mass profile, spatial response and projected deficit](../docs/figures/nsc-atlas-compensation-response.png)

*Figure 25. One supplied core and its inherited reservoir, viewed through
enclosed mass, spatial frequency and projected contrast. Including the
reservoir suppresses long-wavelength response. The purple curve reuses
the evolved field in the original mass/radius units. The projected deficit
is a spatial statistic; a relativistic lens observation needs its own
averaging and light-cone dictionary.*

### 14.15. The age and population are part of the correlation

The inherited vacuum remains fixed while the surrounding field evolves.
Postprocessing the five authenticated spherical evolution checkpoints,
with physical radius $R=Ax/(\omega\zeta)$, gives at slow time $600$:

| Spatial quantity | Fine result and comparison range |
| --- | ---: |
| $k_{1/2}R_{d,0}$ | $3.165445\pm.000031$ |
| $\Lambda_v/(M_{c,\mathrm{current}}k_{1/2}^3)$ | $.559473\pm.000024$ |
| Quadratic coefficient in original mass/radius units | $.0998276\pm.0000072$ |

The ranges include grid, time and outer-domain comparisons from the saved
source; no evolution was rerun. This changes the initial coefficient by
much more than numerical error. An age-independent interpretation of
$.2681493$ is excluded for this history. Long-time full-relativistic
accuracy remains outside the established envelope calculation.

The finite-domain total excess agrees with initial excess minus the outer
mass flux to better than $3.5\times10^{-9}$ of the initial core mass.
Its small nonzero monopole is not resolved against outer-domain variation.
Core accretion is not total mass creation. The turnover is nevertheless
resolved, and neither exact compensation at the original $R_d$ nor a strict
evolved zero-wave-number power law has been imposed.

For a spatial density descriptor $h=M_R/(4\pi R^2)$, projection
along a line of sight gives

$$
\Sigma(b)=2\int_b^{R_d}\frac{h(R)R\,dR}{\sqrt{R^2-b^2}}.
$$

Its planar Fourier transform equals the three-dimensional transform at
zero line-of-sight wave number. The same compensation scale therefore
appears in projection. Outside the core the initial example has negative
surface contrast but positive mean-minus-local contrast, both vanishing
outside $R_d$. A weak-field lens interpretation would connect those two
quantities to convergence and tangential shear. It requires the averaged
source and null-propagation approximation; the exact instantaneous scalar
optics cannot be obtained by renaming this projection a lens map.

The correlation framework is established in
[Schmidt, 1511.02231v2](https://arxiv.org/html/1511.02231v2) and
[Chen--Afshordi, 1912.04872v5, Section III](https://arxiv.org/html/1912.04872v5).
The contribution here is a calculated compensated kernel from the inherited
source, its mass--vacuum scale relation and its measured age dependence.
For identical local profiles with number-density fluctuation $\Delta n$,

$$
\delta_{\rm red}(k)=\frac{M_c}{\rho_g}
W(kR_d)\Delta n(k),\qquad
P_{\rm red}(k)=\left(\frac{M_c}{\rho_g}\right)^2
|W(kR_d)|^2P_n(k).
$$

Here $\rho_g$ is mean geometric-mass density, and $P_n$ is the spectrum
of absolute number-density fluctuations. Bounded $P_n$ gives an initial
$O(k^4)$ redistribution spectrum; independent centres have $P_n=n_{\mathrm{mean}}$.
The total matter spectrum also contains the large-scale field and its
cross correlations. Suppressing that field would discard the very signal
one wants to explain. No abundance, formation law or matter fraction has
been calculated by this single-profile construction.

The next connection is consequently formation and age selection in the
inherited parent, followed by the observation map for that population.
The atlas supplies a joint spatial relation to test when those inputs
exist; it does not fix them by matching a desired sky spectrum.

### 14.16. Does the inherited parent leave enough time to form a core?

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-formation-window -->

Derivation and calculation: [formation budget and compact seed](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-formation-window-v1.json)
and [all-wavelength comparison proof](../docs/nsc-atlas-formation-window.md).

Persistence of an installed core does not explain its formation. The
earlier diffuse-cloud example also began with a nonlinear overdensity.
Before another collapse grid, ask how much a small ambient disturbance
can grow in the same inherited parent. Keep $\omega=10^9$,
$\Lambda=9/32$, $D=\kappa E_b=3/8$ and the retained curvature $C$:

$$
H^2=\frac{\Lambda}{3}+\frac{D}{3A^3}-\frac{C}{A^2}.
$$

Linearizing the existing classical envelope gives density contrast
$\delta$ and phase $\theta$. For local comoving wave number $k$,

$$
\dot\delta=\frac{k^2\theta}{\omega A^2},\qquad
\dot\theta=-\frac{k^2\delta}{4\omega A^2}
+\frac{\omega D\delta}{2Ak^2},
$$
$$
\ddot\delta+2H\dot\delta+
\left(\frac{k^4}{4\omega^2A^4}-\frac{D}{2A^3}\right)\delta=0.
$$

The related averaged massive-scalar equation is given by
[Hwang--Noh, 0902.4738, equations (19)--(28)](https://arxiv.org/html/0902.4738).
Here it follows from the already declared local envelope with the actual
inherited expansion. This is not a global flat-parent approximation or a
new quantum postulate. An initially real envelope has zero current;
a growing-mode velocity would be additional preparation data.

There is no need to guess a sufficiently late stopping time. Set
$u=1/A$, $\nu=k^2/(2\omega)$ and introduce the rescaled density velocity:

$$
p=A^2\dot\delta,\qquad
\delta_u=-\frac{up}{H(u)},\qquad
p_u=\frac{\nu^2u-D/2}{H(u)}\delta.
$$

The entire expanding future ends at the regular point $u=0$, because
$H$ stays positive and tends to the vacuum expansion rate. The transfer matrix
in $(\delta,p)$ has determinant one, providing an independent check.
Regularity of this reduced endpoint does not establish infinite-time
agreement with full GR or complete the unresolved wall future.

To bound every wavelength, use kinetic time $d\eta=dt/A^2$. The equation
becomes a forced oscillator with nonnegative feedback:

$$
\delta_{\eta\eta}+\nu^2\delta=\frac{DA}{2}\delta.
$$

The free Green kernel obeys $|\sin[\nu(\eta-s)]/\nu|\leq\eta-s$.
Iterating the positive Volterra comparison therefore bounds the absolute
solution by the zero-frequency density and velocity responses. This proves
the bound even when a finite-wave-number solution oscillates or changes
sign; simply deleting its pressure term would not prove that statement.

For zero initial current, an elementary Gronwall estimate gives

$$
|\delta_k|\leq
\exp\left(\frac{D u_i^3}{12H_{\min}^2}\right)|\delta_i|.
$$

At $A_i=1$, this factor is $1.39616912$, below $1.397$. Solving the
positive comparison gives the sharper evaluated relation

$$
|\delta_k(t)|\leq1.26934144123|\delta_i|
+0.68728524662|v_i|,\qquad v_i=\dot\delta_i/H_i.
$$

The exact comparison solutions define the coefficients; the printed
decimals have numerical comparison ranges below $3.4\times10^{-12}$,
not certified interval endpoints. The independent pressureless basis
$H(A)$ and its reduction-of-order partner reproduces the result within
$8\times10^{-15}$. The standard basis is attributed to
[Heath (1977)](https://doi.org/10.1093/mnras/179.3.351); its governing
identity is verified directly here.

Thus a small zero-current disturbance introduced at this late anchor
cannot reach nonlinear contrast through the stated linear mechanism,
even if evolved forever. This conclusion is specific to its epoch,
initial current and equations. Earlier seeds, forcing or already nonlinear
data are different possibilities. The bound also applies to spatial L2
norm; a pointwise statement needs an appropriate Fourier norm.

### 14.17. A compact earlier seed identifies the nonlinear continuation

Construct a local compensated disturbance without the stationary core:

$$
g(r)=(1-r^2/L^2)^4_+,\qquad
\delta_i(r)=\epsilon\frac{-\Delta(g*g)(r)}
{\int|\nabla g|^2d^3x}.
$$

It has support $2L$, zero total density contrast, central value $\epsilon$
and nonnegative Fourier transform proportional to $k^2|G(k)|^2$, where
$G$ is the Fourier transform of $g$.
Consequently $|\delta_i(r)|\leq\epsilon$, and the mode comparison gives
a pointwise future bound for this entire seed. Its linear Poisson potential
is compact as well. These are smooth local-envelope data, not yet a new
exact nonlinear shared-wall preparation.

Take $L=.000481562379847$, the previous supply radius interpreted as a
comoving length, a supplied peak contrast of $5%$, and zero initial phase.
At $A_i=1$ the central future gain is $1.26488829$; the global pointwise
bound keeps the contrast below about $6.35%$.

Now place that same dimensionless seed at $A_i=.01$ on the earlier
averaged-parent continuation. Its central linear gain is

$$
35.609032\pm.000004.
$$

The stated range combines an analytic spectral-tail bound with a numerical
integration comparison. No truncated spectrum is renormalized. For
$q=kL$, the normalized bump transform is $10395j_5(q)/q^5$; Parseval fixes
the density-weight integral, while the explicit sine/cosine polynomial for
$j_5$ bounds its omitted tail. At $q=60$ the omitted pointwise gain is at
most $3.07\times10^{-6}$ for all future times. Quadrature and ODE comparisons
add about $1.02\times10^{-8}$.

The supplied $5%$ seed reaches linear contrast $.1$ near $A=.0957400$.
Its formal linear extrapolation reaches contrast one near $A=1.0190483$.
That second event is outside linear validity: it identifies the nonlinear
continuation to calculate, not a completed collapse or a formed core.
The late-parent bound and the earlier amplification together show why
the preparation epoch is a physical part of the proposed formation route.

### 14.18. Nonlinear formation without installing the final core

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-seed-collapse -->

Derivation and calculation: [nonlinear compact-seed comparison](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-seed-collapse-v1.json)
and [initial constraints, boundary clock and balances](../docs/nsc-atlas-seed-collapse.md).

The compact disturbance has a finite-amplitude continuation. Keep the
same action, inherited vacuum and excitation, comoving length and earlier
epoch. The $5%$ seed is evolved to $A=16$. A separately declared $10%$
preparation is tested through its first compression and rebound at $A=1.2$.
This amplitude comparison is not a fit to an observed object or an inferred
universal formation threshold. No stationary core is placed in either seed.

The autocorrelation source from Section 14.17 has an exact factored
real-space polynomial. Its radial mass primitive is positive inside the
support and vanishes at its edge. Normalize the total envelope by its
homogeneous value and set

$$
f_i(r)=\sqrt{1+\epsilon S(r)-\lambda W(r)},\qquad
r=\frac{R}{AL}.
$$

The square root preserves the intended finite density contrast. The fixed
smooth bump $W$ occupies the outer part of the original support. Its
coefficient is solved from the full local initial gravitational constraint,
not chosen to force a later collapse. Let $M_*=DL^3/2$, $n=M/M_*$,
$q=1/(\omega^2 A_i^2L^2)$, $c=CL^2$ and $\gamma=DL^2/A_i$. Then

$$
n_r=r^2\left[\epsilon S-\lambda W+qB(f_i')^2\right],\qquad
B=1-cr^2-\frac{\gamma n}{r},\qquad n(0)=n(2)=0.
$$

Without the correction, positive scalar gradient energy leaves a small
exterior mass offset. The derivative of the zero-gradient endpoint with
respect to $\lambda$ is negative, so a nearby compensating branch exists.
The solved values are $9.75930\times10^{-12}$ and $3.85046\times10^{-11}$
for the two amplitudes. An independent proper-distance Hamilton solve
checks the endpoint, and the initial radial metric remains positive.
This constructs local turning-point Einstein-scalar data in the declared
earlier parent patch. It does not construct the earlier material walls.

The envelope omits that gradient contribution, so its small initial mass
residual is retained in the subsequent balance. It also retains the stated
carrier-quadrature difference from exact zero scalar momentum. These are
identified approximation differences, not silently removed inputs.

The nonlinear evolution uses logarithmic scale time $N=\ln A$:

$$
i f_N=-\frac{a}{2}\Delta f+bVf,\qquad
\Delta V=\frac{|f|^2-1}{2},
$$
$$
a=\frac{1}{\omega L^2A^2H},\qquad
b=\frac{\omega DL^2}{AH}.
$$

The finite boundary field and its gravitational clock must agree. A fixed
boundary phase uses $V(L_{\rm box})=0$, implemented by subtracting the
free-space potential's boundary value. This leaves bulk spatial forces
unchanged and preserves a symmetric Green kernel. An initially inconsistent
boundary choice failed conservation before nonlinear growth and is rejected.
The corrected kernel passes an independent radial eigenfunction test.

The existing sine kinetic map and full-field potential kicks are retained.
Integrated time coefficients follow the early expansion, and a potential
phase limit resolves the first rapid compression. With the physical-time
Hamiltonian split into kinetic $T$ and self-gravitational $W$, the exact
expansion identity is

$$
\frac{d(T+W)}{d\ln A}=-(2T+W).
$$

Mass transport is checked at fixed comoving cuts and the outer boundary.
The work integral is evaluated every step. Three spatial resolutions,
an independent time control and a larger domain test the $10%$ history.
The $5%$ history has its own three resolutions, with no-gravity and
small-amplitude controls completing eleven runs.

Track a shell carrying a fixed amount of the oscillatory scalar source:

\qquad R_m(N)=ALr_m(N).
$$

This is an exact mass label in the envelope continuity equation. It
excludes vacuum volume mass and higher-order GR gradient corrections.
A decreasing comoving radius alone is insufficient: the physical radius
must turn around and contract.

For the $10%$ preparation, the shell initially at $r=.25$ turns around

| Selected shell physical radius | $(4.38056\pm.03788)\times10^{-6}$ |
| Selected shell radius / largest sampled radius | $.109898\pm.000950$ |

The selected mass contracts by about $89%$ from its largest sampled

## V. The interior observer: motion, light and expansion

An interior observer tests a geometry through clocks, trajectories and light. This part uses the sources already constructed to derive frequency relations, focusing, multiple images and motion-calibrated optical predictions. Endpoint identities connect measurements that belong to one geometry. The final cosmological comparison confronts a quiet limiting state with actual supernova data, identifying the expansion history that the full account must improve.

### 15. One geometry, several measured consequences

The following sections give the worked constructions and their attributed mathematical inputs.

### 15.1. Three freely falling clocks and a withheld prediction

<!-- nsc-claim:three-clock-parent-relation -->

Let $L_{\rm ref}=a_0$ and initialize three ideal test emitters at
proper separations $(.04,.08,.12)L_{\rm ref}$ from the parent pole.
They initially rest relative to the slice normal, emit continuously
at known rest frequencies, and freely fall without backreaction.
The central free-fall receiver observes at fixed proper age
2.251557477076, corresponding to background N=.8. All rays remain
inside the protected parent domain.

Observer terms are essential in relativistic measured quantities;
see [Scaccabarozzi, Yoo and Biern](https://arxiv.org/html/1807.09796).
Here the actual closed-parent metric supplies the calculation.
Holding initial proper distance and receiver age fixed gives

$$
\delta\theta_s(0)=\int_0^s\Phi(0,\theta)d\theta,
\qquad \delta t_o=-\int_0^{t_o}\Phi(t,0)dt.
$$

The source's outward peculiar velocity obeys

$$
\dot v_s+Hv_s=-\frac{\partial_\theta\Phi(t,s)}{a},
\qquad \delta\dot\theta_s=v_s/a.
$$

Along the unperturbed incoming ray, $\theta=\eta_o-\eta(t)$. The perturbed
null equation sets the emission-time correction,

$$
\delta t_e=a_e\left[\frac{\delta t_o}{a_o}
 +2\int_{t_e}^{t_o}\frac{\Phi}{a}dt-\delta\theta_s(t_e)\right].
$$

The measured log-frequency departure from the background is

$$
y=\log\frac{(\nu_o/\nu_e)}{a_e/a_o}
=H_e\delta t_e-H_o\delta t_o+\Phi_e-\Phi_o-v_e
 +2\int_{t_e}^{t_o}\partial_t\Phi\,dt.
$$

The last derivative is partial at fixed position. Source motion,
potential, propagation and clock contributions all enter; no
instantaneous potential difference alone determines the measurement.

Writing $y_i=R_{i1}\alpha+R_{i2}\beta$ for the two retained amplitudes,
the computed response is:

| Initial distance / $L_{\rm ref}$ | Background frequency ratio | Density column | Phase column |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| .04 | .840672581087 | .017861103048 | -.000731094396 |
| .08 | .721722111018 | .030644152339 | -.001671366286 |
| .12 | .628648035135 | .038548233209 | -.002804913120 |

The first two rows have nonzero determinant and condition number
169.341 in this amplitude normalization. Eliminating both amplitudes
gives the conditional prediction

$$
y_3=-2.88990465\,y_1+2.94232704\,y_2.
$$

This is [NSC-P003](../docs/prediction-register.md), registered
retrospectively without astronomical data. In the synthetic check,
amplitudes $(10^{-5},-4\times10^{-6})$ give departures
$(1.81535408,3.13126989,3.96701985)\times10^{-7}$. Calibrating only
the first two recovers the amplitudes and predicts the withheld third.

![Operational three-clock protocol and synthetic test](../docs/figures/nsc-atlas-parent-clocks.png)

*Figure 28. Left: three emitters on the central past radial light cone;
their initial proper distances and the receiver's proper age are fixed.
Right: a withheld synthetic frequency response agrees with the prediction
from two state calibrations. No observational confirmation is inferred.*

An independent check integrates timelike sources and radial photons
in finite metric deformations, fixing the physical distances and
proper age by direct integration. The null Hamilton equation is

$$
\frac{d\log(-p_t)}{dt}
=\frac{\partial_tA}{2A}-\frac{\partial_tB}{2B},
\qquad A=1+2\Phi,\quad B=a^2(1-2\Phi).
$$

Symmetric metric variations verify the linear clock response;
the mixed-direction derivative differs by $5.23\times10^{-11}$
at variation step .0005. The finite metrics validate the first
variation, not a nonlinear Einstein-scalar solution. Refinement
changes the null weights by $1.35\times10^{-9}$; the retained
coefficient comparison tolerance is $10^{-7}$.

For independent equal frequency-log errors $\sigma_y$, the null residual
has uncertainty $4.243682044\sigma_y$. A full covariance must replace
that example for correlated measurements. Unknown source velocities,
an arbitrary spatial profile or extra fields would add parameters
and need not obey the three-clock relation. The
[derivation](../docs/nsc-atlas-parent-clocks.md) and
[compact evidence](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-parent-clocks-v1.json)
keep those restrictions explicit. The advance is an operational test
surviving the two unknown state amplitudes, while physical formation
and an unrestricted primordial spectrum remain open.

### 15.2. A signal from the concentration's evolving geometry

<!-- nsc-claim:formed-core-clock -->

The central emitter is geodesic. The receiver is actively held at areal
radius .6 and starts its proper clock on the initial slice. In the diffuse
case the emission occurs at central proper age 4, after the contraction
has developed. Reception time is determined by propagation, not chosen
as an equal-coordinate-time comparison.

An outgoing photon with frequency E measured by the slice normal obeys

$$
\frac{dR_\gamma}{dt}=\alpha(HR_\gamma+\sqrt{B}),\qquad
\frac{d\log E}{dt}=\alpha K_r-\sqrt{B}\alpha_R.
$$

At reception, the held-radius observer's velocity relative to that normal
adds a necessary Doppler factor:

$$
\frac{\nu_o}{\nu_e}=\frac{E_o}{E_e}
\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{B_o}+H_oR_o}{\sqrt{B_o}-H_oR_o}},
\qquad
\frac{d\tau_o}{dt}=\alpha_o
\sqrt{1-\frac{H_o^2R_o^2}{B_o}}.
$$

An independent canonical null-geodesic calculation transports $p_R$ with
Hamiltonian $\alpha(HR+\sqrt{B})p_R$. It is checked against this normal-frame
formula. A separate unaveraged homogeneous integration gives the
corresponding control signal analytically along its FLRW light cone.

For the diffuse run emitted at central proper age 4, the fractional
frequency departure from the homogeneous comparison is

$$
\frac{(\nu_o/\nu_e)}{(\nu_o/\nu_e)_{\rm hom}}-1
=-0.0041375586\pm 1.1\times10^{-8}.
$$

This is approximately -0.413756 percent, with a finite numerical error
estimate of .0000011 percentage points. The receiver's additional proper
arrival time is .01703546, with numerical comparison error 2.2e-7 in atlas
time units. Both the emitter's accumulated proper clock and propagation
contribute to this delay. The independent canonical frequency differs by
less than 4.7e-11 on the finest run.

The coherent control, emitted at central age 0, has frequency departure
-.007456287 with numerical comparison error 2.1e-7 and proper arrival delay
.000476466. These use different declared emission ages; they are not a
same-epoch comparison of the two states. All errors here concern numerical
resolution and event calculation, not physical preparation uncertainty.


The frequency departures are dimensionless. Delays are in atlas time units;
an independent physical length would convert them to seconds. These
conditional observables do not import P003's earlier linear coefficients
or select a primordial population. The
[complete protocol](../docs/nsc-atlas-core-evolution.md) and
[numerical comparisons](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-core-evolution-v1.json)
give the emission events, receiver motion, error estimates and model scope.

The connection now runs from a declared inherited scalar preparation,
through self-consistent local evolution, to a received signal. The parent

observed scale, explaining the population of structures and testing their

### 15.3. The same concentration bends and focuses light

<!-- nsc-claim:evolved-core-null-focusing -->

The radial clock is followed by a nonradial optical test on the same
evolved scalar geometry. The field stepper and its physical inputs remain
unchanged; final-state hashes must reproduce the preceding source calculation
at every retained resolution. The lens is not replaced by a fitted density
profile or a static metric.

Scalar lenses have published precedents in
[Dabrowski and Schunck](https://arxiv.org/abs/astro-ph/9807039), and
[Aimar et al.](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23800) study time-dependent
real-scalar optical signatures. The contribution here is the connection
to the computed inherited parent and its formation history. The exact
lens-map approach follows
[Frittelli and Newman](https://arxiv.org/html/gr-qc/9810017), with the
observer-screen formulation made explicit below.

The canonical bulk equation gives an exact null contraction:

$$
R_{ab}=\kappa(\nabla_a\phi\nabla_b\phi+Ug_{ab}),
\qquad R_{ab}k^ak^b=\kappa(k\phi)^2.
$$

The potential, including the inherited vacuum, drops out of this direct
focusing term. It still affects the field and spacetime through which the
ray travels. Its absence from this contraction is not invariance of the
whole optical experiment under a change of Lambda.

For normal energy E, radial momentum $q=\sqrt{B}p_R$ and conserved angular
momentum L, the two principal tidal eigenvalues are

$$
T_\perp=\frac{\kappa}{2}(E\Pi+q\sqrt{B}F)^2
+\frac{3WL^2}{R^2},\qquad
T_\parallel=\frac{\kappa}{2}(E\Pi+q\sqrt{B}F)^2
-\frac{3WL^2}{R^2},
$$

where

$$
W=\frac{m}{R^3}-\frac{\kappa}{6}
\left(U+\frac{\Pi^2-BF^2}{2}\right).
$$

Ricci curvature focuses both directions. Weyl curvature changes them with
opposite signs, producing optical shear. The spherical Einstein Hessians
and warped-product Riemann tensor independently give these factors and
signs. In the homogeneous parent W vanishes. At a pure vacuum extremum
both tidal eigenvalues vanish, although expansion and distances still
depend on the vacuum scale.

The principal Jacobi scales obey

$$
\frac{d^2D_i}{d\lambda^2}=-T_iD_i,
\qquad D_i(0)=0,\qquad D_i'(0)=1.
$$

This is the attributed optical transport framework of
[Seitz, Schneider and Ehlers](https://arxiv.org/abs/astro-ph/9403056),
applied to the current source. The
[curvature derivation](../docs/nsc-atlas-core-optics.md) and
[compact calculation](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-core-optics-v1.json)
separate the exact identities from finite numerical lensing evidence.

### 15.5. Two paths, one receiving clock

A lens map becomes an image prediction when the emitting and receiving
worldlines are fixed. Keep the scalar-parent solution of the preceding
sections. Hold both worldlines at areal radius $R_s=.6$, put the emitter at
azimuth zero and the receiver at $\pi-b$, with $b=.0002$. A pulse leaves at
emitter proper age $2.8$. If $\Phi(\psi,\tau_e)$ is the positive-orientation
landing map, the two selected branches satisfy

$$
\Phi(\psi_+,\tau_e)=\pi-b,\qquad
\Phi(\psi_-,\tau_e)=\pi+b.
$$

Reflection sends the second ray to $-(\pi+b)$, which is the same receiving
worldline modulo $2\pi$. The images arrive at different proper times on
that one observer's clock. This improves on a fixed-launch-angle comparison,
where the landing observer itself can change. The two selected angular
patches do not constitute a census of all possible images.

A null geodesic's endpoint variation relates these image measurements.
Normalize its emitted energy to one. The null action gives
$p_a\delta x_o^a-p_a\delta x_e^a=0$. The emitter is spatially fixed;
the held receiving sphere contributes the local energy and conserved
angular momentum $L$. Hence

$$
d\tau_o=\frac{d\tau_e}{\nu_o}
+\frac{L}{\nu_o}\,d\varphi_o.
$$

This equation does not assume a static lens or a conserved photon energy.
It uses the actual evolving metric and endpoint observer tetrads. At a
fixed receiving worldline, each image obeys $d\tau_i/d\tau_e=1/\nu_i$.
For the relative delay $\Delta=\tau_--\tau_+$,

$$
\frac{d\Delta}{d\tau_e}=\frac{1}{\nu_-}-\frac{1}{\nu_+},\qquad
\frac{d\Delta}{d\tau_+}=\frac{\nu_+}{\nu_-}-1.
$$

The second expression requires only the receiver's clock and the ratio
of frequencies of matched source features. Image delays and frequency
shifts are linked consequences of one geometry. The general endpoint and
lens-map ideas are established mathematics; their evaluated connection
to the forming source in this atlas is the present result.

Conserved angular momentum also fixes the received image directions:
$\sin|\xi_i|=\sin\psi_i/\nu_i$. Choose positive sky direction opposite
increasing azimuth, so that $\xi_+>0$ and $\xi_-<0$. For a common,
infinitesimal isotropic pulse, its observed bolometric fluence is

$$
\mathcal F_i=\frac{E_e}{4\pi}
\frac{\nu_i}{|D_{\perp,i}D_{\parallel,i}|}.
$$

A continuous source instead gives a flux proportional to
$\nu_i^2/|D_{\perp,i}D_{\parallel,i}|$, because reception rate adds a
second frequency factor. The signed determinant retains image parity.
These relations assume transparent geometric optics and a detector
perpendicular to the arriving ray. Finite source size, duration and
wavelength require separate modeling.

The [fixed-worldline derivation](../docs/nsc-atlas-core-images.md) gives
its endpoint proof, angular inverse and independent checks. Source ages
$2.8\pm.0002$ and $2.8\pm.0001$ test the delay derivative; curvature
and angular resampling test the two image inverses. All source evolution
files remain unchanged.

The completed pair is resolved at three grid spacings, with an additional
optical-time comparison. Its conditional observables are:

| Observable | Calculated value | Numerical comparison range |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Image separation | 0.682670 degrees | 0.000493 degrees |
| Relative proper arrival delay | 1.44125317e-6 atlas units | 9.54e-10 atlas units |
| Negative/positive frequency | 1.00000922695 | 8.52e-9 |
| Negative/positive pulse fluence | 1.401958 | 0.006483 |

The negative-parity image is brighter and arrives later. The rotation/Jacobi
error decreases from $5.65\times10^{-4}$ to $3.35\times10^{-5}$ to
$1.92\times10^{-6}$. The angular inverse and its curvature-based check
agree to better than $1.31\times10^{-10}$ radians on the fine patches.
All four field replays match the previously retained solutions exactly.
The [compact evidence](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-core-images-v1.json)
records the fixed inputs, individual images and numerical comparisons.

The directly differentiated delay agrees with the inverse-frequency
identity to $1.55\times10^{-8}$ per source proper time, within its

### 15.6. A local image delay and the parent's vacuum must share one scale

The local concentration belongs to the same sourced parent whose protected
annulus retains the inherited vacuum. For this declared action and flux,
the vacuum and reference radius in atlas units are

$$
\widehat\Lambda=\kappa U_{\rm vac}=9/32,\qquad
\widehat L_{\rm ref}=6.62607707610.
$$

Thus the earlier conditional result
$\Lambda L_{\rm ref}^2=12.34825239893$ still applies.

Let $L_{\rm unit}$ be the physical length assigned to one atlas unit and
let $\delta$ be the computed dimensionless image delay. Any consistent
dimensional embedding must obey

$$
\Lambda_{\rm phys}=\frac{\widehat\Lambda}{L_{\rm unit}^2},\qquad
\Delta t=\frac{L_{\rm unit}}{c}\,\delta,
$$

and therefore

$$
\Lambda_{\rm phys}(c\Delta t)^2=\widehat\Lambda\,\delta^2.
$$

For the computed pair,

$$
\Lambda_{\rm phys}(c\Delta t)^2
=(5.8422\pm.0078)\times10^{-13}.
$$

The quoted range propagates the numerical delay envelope while holding
the declared parent vacuum fixed. It does not include uncertainty in a
physical interpretation or an unspecified preparation law.

The free unit cancels. Calibrating a physical scale with an image delay
leaves the vacuum value, angular image separation and frequency/fluence
ratios as linked tests. Matching each by an independent scale choice
would break this connection. This does not select the physical scale or
prove that the inherited vacuum equals the value inferred from cosmology.
It creates a precise joint requirement on that proposed identification.

The cloud, emitting age and receiver position remain specified inputs.
Population selection and nonspherical persistence remain necessary for an
astronomical application. The atlas provides the mathematical route
from inherited vacuum, through formed structure, to a fixed observer's
measurements without an independently fitted lens mass.

### 15.7. The forming field supplies one connected observer map

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-formed-observer -->

Derivation and calculation: [fixed-observer response](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-formed-observer-v1.json)
and [metric, mass-current and endpoint derivation](../docs/nsc-atlas-formed-observer.md).

The finite concentration can now be used as a source for light. Start from
the authenticated $A=1.2$ fields of the preceding formation calculation.
Short backward and forward steps reconstruct a local interval around that
event using the same field operators; no formation grid or fitted halo
profile is substituted. The source's time dependence remains in the ray.

The declared averaged weak metric is

$$
ds^2=-(1+2\Phi)dt^2+A^2L^2(1-2\Phi)
\left(\frac{dr^2}{B_0}+r^2d\Omega^2\right),\qquad
B_0=1-CL^2r^2,\qquad \Phi=\frac{DL^2}{A}V.
$$

It retains the parent spatial curvature and equal leading scalar
potentials. Null propagation is solved in this metric. Omitted higher-order
Einstein-scalar, carrier and stress terms remain approximation errors;
the numerical ranges below are not bounds on the full relativistic model.

There is a direct connection between the field current and the potential's
time derivative. Let $N=\ln A$, $j=\operatorname{Im}(f^*f_r)$ and
$m(r)=\int_0^r s^2(|f|^2-1)ds$. The existing envelope continuity equation
gives $m_N=-ar^2j$, with $a=1/(\omega L^2A^2H)$. Since the boundary-clock
potential is an integral of $m/r^2$,

$$
\Phi_r=\frac{DL^2}{2A}\frac{m(r)}{r^2},\qquad
\Phi_N=-\Phi+\frac{DL^2a}{2A}\int_r^{L_{\rm box}}j(s)ds.
$$

The potential, its spatial force and its time variation therefore come
from the same formed source. Spectral field derivatives and exact radial
polynomial integrals evaluate them; no derivative of a stored plot is used.
A Hermite interpolation in time preserves a consistent metric and its
derivatives. Pure-phase data give an independent analytic check of the
current term.

Place test clocks at comoving radius $r_s=.25$, with the emitter at
azimuth zero and receiver at $\Theta=\pi-.08$. They are held at these
coordinates. They do not follow the scalar flow or the contracting mass
shell that began at $r=.25$ in the earlier preparation. Their proper-clock
rate is

$$
\frac{d\tau_s}{dN}=\frac{\sqrt{1+2\Phi(r_s,N)}}{H(N)}.
$$

Set both clocks to zero on the declared initial slab slice. The emission
proper age equals the homogeneous reference clock's age at $A=1.2$.
Solve its actual emission event in the perturbed metric, then shoot the
inward angle to the fixed receiving worldline. This matches proper age
and receiver position, rather than comparing different arrival locations.
It resolves one weak-image branch in the declared angular bracket.

With $dq=dt/(AL)$, the null Hamiltonian is

$$
\mathcal{H}_q=F\sqrt{B_0p^2+J^2/r^2},\qquad
F=\sqrt{\frac{1+2\Phi}{1-2\Phi}}.
$$

The emitted frequency is normalized to one. Its received value obeys

$$
\log\frac{\nu_o}{\nu_e}=N_e-N_o+
\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{1+2\Phi_e}{1+2\Phi_o}
+\int\frac{2\Phi_NN_q}{1-4\Phi^2}dq.
$$

The small time-dependent term is integrated directly, avoiding cancellation
between nearly equal photon energies. An endpoint energy identity checks
it independently. The receiver's proper flight time is also integrated;
it differs from a coordinate Shapiro delay.

### 15.8. Direction, delay and frequency are derivatives of one arrival map

Let $T(\tau_e,\Theta)=\tau_o$ be the arrival map. Null endpoint variation
gives, for the specified worldlines,

$$
\nu_o\,d\tau_o=\nu_e\,d\tau_e+J_{\rm physical}\,d\Theta.
$$

For unit emitted frequency this implies

$$
\frac{\partial T}{\partial\tau_e}=\frac{1}{\nu_o},\qquad
\frac{\partial T}{\partial\Theta}=R_o\sin\beta_o,
\qquad R_o=A_oLr_s\sqrt{1-2\Phi_o}.
$$

Here $\beta_o$ is the observed ray angle relative to the radial axis.
Subtracting the homogeneous reference gives two independently testable
relations for the excess delay. They belong to the general spacetime
Fermat/endpoint framework described by
[Perlick](https://arxiv.org/abs/1010.3416) and
[Fleury--Larena--Uzan](https://arxiv.org/html/2011.04440v4).
The contribution here is their evaluated application to the actual forming
source. The identities check one connected prediction; they are not three
independent confirmations of nesting.

For the primary fixed observer, the result is:

| Readout relative to the matched homogeneous parent | Fine result and numerical comparison range |
| --- | ---: |
| Image-direction change | $21.20\pm.20$ milliarcseconds |
| Proper flight-time excess, with the supplied ruler | $27.5906\pm.0120$ days |
| Log received-frequency change | $(-1.44088\pm.01399)\times10^{-11}$ |

The delay conversion uses the previously supplied
$\Lambda=10^{-52}\,\mathrm{m}^{-2}$ ruler; the angular conversion does
not need an SI length. Source parameters, clock protocol and geometry are
specified, and no astronomical data were fitted. The same calculation
also fixes a scale-independent relation:

$$
\Lambda_{\rm physical}(c\,\Delta\tau)^2
=(5.10730\pm.00441)\times10^{-23}.
$$

This connects the inherited vacuum to a local flight-time excess within
the declared model. It does not determine the observed absolute vacuum.

### 15.9. Motion calibrates a prediction before the new ray is evaluated

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-dynamics-lensing -->

Derivation and calculation: [frozen NSC-P004 forecast](../results/summaries/nsc-p004-dynamics-lensing-forecast-v1.json),
[separate validation](../results/summaries/nsc-p004-dynamics-lensing-validation-v1.json)
and [derivation and preparation](../docs/nsc-atlas-dynamics-lensing.md).

The preceding result supplied light propagation from a known forming
source. The next test reverses part of that inference: calibrate its force
from finite timelike motion and predict a new optical response before
computing the corresponding null ray. The source, weak metric and action
remain unchanged.

Preparation first checks whether an equilibrium rotation curve is justified.
For the source acceleration $g$ at fixed physical radius, compare its
variation rate with $\Omega^2=g/R$. The instantaneous ratios
$|\partial_t\log g|/\Omega$ are about $.611$ near $r=.0073$ and $.457$
near $r=.015$. They do not support slow variation over an orbital period.
This is a timescale diagnostic, not a theorem excluding every orbit.
The selected method therefore uses short freely falling motions.

For positive lapse $\alpha^2=1+2\Phi$, spatial scale
$s^2=A^2L^2(1-2\Phi)$ and $B=1-CL^2r^2$, a radial unit-mass test body has

$$
\mathcal{H}_t=\alpha\sqrt{1+BP^2/s^2}.
$$

Its trajectory and proper clock follow the timelike Hamilton equations.
At release with zero peculiar velocity, the local radial velocity relative
to the stated cosmological frame obeys

$$
-\left.\frac{dv}{d\tau}\right|_0
=\frac{\sqrt{B}\,\Phi_r}
{AL\sqrt{1-2\Phi}(1+2\Phi)}.
$$

The test body's proper four-acceleration is zero. This is a relative
velocity change, measured in the specified reference frames.
Centered before/after velocities reconstruct the leading force:

$$
\Phi_r^{\rm rec}=-\frac{AL}{\sqrt{B}}
\frac{v_+-v_-}{\tau_+-\tau_-}.
$$

The refined calibration uses $96$ probes on the incoming and outgoing
halves of the retarded background chord. Their central release events,
proper-time intervals and initial velocities are specified. The initial
source and receiver themselves are another pair of radial geodesics,
released at the slab start. They have the same trajectory at a common
time, but different radii at emission and reception.

The new receiver is at $\Theta=\pi-.12$, absent from the earlier optical
fan. Let its reference chord have impact parameter $b$, length $d$ and
coordinate endpoints $z_e,z_o$. A fixed-endpoint Born calculation gives

$$
\Delta\beta_{\rm lens}
=\frac{2}{d}\int_{z_e}^{z_o}(z-z_e)
\Phi_r^{\rm rec}(N(z),r(z))\frac{b}{r(z)}dz.
$$

The endpoint weight follows by integrating the transverse displacement
equation with both positions fixed. It differs from the total deflection
of a freely specified incoming ray. The retarded times come from the
background chord, not from the withheld optical path.

Calibrated endpoint positions fix the geometric direction. The measured
receiver velocity supplies the exact radial aberration:

$$
\cos\beta_{\rm obs}=
\frac{\cos\beta_{\rm normal}-v_o}
{1-v_o\cos\beta_{\rm normal}}.
$$

The prediction function consumes these motion measurements and geometric
inputs only. It does not read the source potential or interpolate the old
optical fan, and it does not fit an independent lens mass. Equal leading
metric potentials remain a premise of this model. Combining dynamics and
lensing is established practice, illustrated in a different statistical
setting by [Reyes et al.,1003.2185](https://arxiv.org/abs/1003.2185).

The forecast and comparison tolerance were committed and pushed in

before evaluating the new null boundary problem. The forecast is

| Motion-calibrated forecast | 19.43948287 |

The check passes without changing its prediction, tolerance, source or

not astronomical confirmation or a unique signature of nesting.

The frozen window contains a numerical comparison envelope, a probe-release

The exact endpoint boost is

$$
\log\frac{\nu_o}{\nu_e}
=\log\frac{\nu_{o,\rm normal}}{\nu_{e,\rm normal}}
+\log\frac{\gamma_o(1-v_o\mu_o)}

Here $\mu$ is the local photon direction cosine. The moving-clock arrival

Related frequency and viewing-direction effects are treated by

The calibration assumes known endpoint kinematics. Near the measured
angle, velocity error changes the direction by approximately
$\sin\beta\,\delta v$. Keeping that error alone below the frozen tolerance
requires roughly $4.4$ m/s receiver-velocity precision with other quantities
fixed. Position, distance and release-state errors also need propagation.
No such astronomical calibration is claimed here.

The new connection is a motion profile predicting a withheld light
measurement, with its preparation and possible failure specified first.
The calibration probes the inner chord. An alternative geometry agreeing
there would pass the same test. The next outward comparison should ask
whether the surrounding compensation and inherited scale relation break

### 15.11. Two observer distances, on two metrics

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-supernova-distance -->

Scalar volume averages do not by themselves give an observer a distance.
On the exact zero-source quiet cap the luminosity shape is

$$
\frac{D_L}{c/H}=z(1+z).
$$

The comparison retains 596 released Pantheon+ light curves of 545 distinct
supernovae, excluding calibrators and retaining correlated repeat light
curves. The low band is $0.025\leq z_{\mathrm{HD}}<0.075$ (335 curves);
the high band is $0.15\leq z_{\mathrm{HD}}\leq0.25$ (261 curves). Both
lie inside the declared quiet-cap causal domain. For each curve define

$$
y_i=m_{b,i}^{\mathrm{corr}}
-5\log_{10}[z_{\mathrm{HD},i}(1+z_{\mathrm{HEL},i})].
$$

Generalized least squares fits the two band intercepts jointly using the
selected full statistical and systematic covariance. Their high-minus-low
contrast cancels a common brightness and Hubble scale. The quiet prediction
is zero; the measured contrast is

$$
\Delta=-0.1046180474751224\pm0.014373986469318333
\ \mathrm{mag}.
$$

The estimator was frozen before this evaluation. The catalogue itself was
already public, so the test is a retrospective comparison of one quiet
state, not a blind test and not a falsification of a completed
Nested-Space cosmology. No $\Lambda$CDM parameter was fitted, and no
cosmological constant was supplied to improve the residual. The uncertainty
is conditional on the released covariance and standardization, including
its peculiar-velocity treatment. The
[distance-contrast record](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-distance-contrast-v1.json)
stores the estimator specification, selected-row identity, zero prediction
and this residual.

<!-- nsc-claim:conformal-causal-distances -->

The separate evolving metric also has an explicit null cone. Receiver,
emitters and ideal reflectors sit on fixed-coordinate normal worldlines.
Those worldlines may be accelerated. They are not introduced as
cosmological comoving matter, and a slope of redshift against lookback is
not a measurement of $H_0$. For transparent, photon-conserving geometric
optics, with $N=rQ$, reception $(T_o,x_o)=(0.3,1)$ and past rays
$T_e=T_o-\chi$, $x_e=x_o+\eta\chi$,

$$
1+z=\frac{N_o}{N_e},\qquad
D_A=\frac{r_or_e}{N_o}\int_0^\chi Q(T_o-s,x_o+\eta s)^2\,ds,
$$
$$
D_L=(1+z)^2D_A,\qquad
D_{\mathrm{radar}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{T_o-2\chi}^{T_o}N(T,x_o)\,dT.
$$

The lapse in these formulae is the full product $rQ$. The three
predeclared lookbacks are $\chi=0.025$, $0.075$ and $0.125$, in both
directions. The longest radar trip begins at $T=0.05$, inside the saved
time interval. At $\chi=0.125$ both directions have the same radar distance
$D_{\mathrm{radar}}/L_{\mathrm{ref}}=0.031382863175331$.

| Side at $\chi=0.125$ | Redshift | $D_L/D_{\mathrm{radar}}$ |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| Smaller $x$ | $0.0016677447278268254$ | $1.0000739824887968$ |
| Larger $x$ | $-0.007306104080127329$ | $0.991108506097534$ |

The ratios carry no unit. The largest 32/64-point difference is
$3.34\times10^{-16}$. Across the four stored cases the largest deviations
are $2.94\times10^{-10}$ in redshift and $4.84\times10^{-10}$ in the ratio.
Those figures do not bound interpolation or the total model uncertainty.
The method was frozen at `f0f639d` before the rays were evaluated, with no
new gravitational or field solve. The
[light-cone record](../results/summaries/nsc-conformal-lightcone-v1.json)
contains all six rays. Figure 33 shows the three declared lengths; the lines

## VI. Gravitational exchange, fluid motion and quantum state

The same geometric question extends to gravitational waves, unresolved motion and quantum fields: which boundary and state determine the measured response? A separately specified stable material geometry gives an actual gravitational Schur action and return contrast. Fluid correlations provide a conserved stress mechanism in their own domain. Physical Dirac propagation and the geometry-derived cosmohedron exchange then connect explicit quantum states to sourced geometry. Each mathematical bridge retains its field content and approximation.

### 16. Responses across matter, gravity and quantum descriptions

The following sections give the worked constructions and their attributed mathematical inputs.

### 16.1. Flow geometry and a common boundary stress

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-flow-boundary-dictionary -->

The [flow dictionary](../docs/nsc-atlas-flow-dictionary.md) connects several
published constructions through explicit operations. PG coordinates expose
radial null directions as $dr/dt=v\pm1$. Linear potential disturbances in a
prepared barotropic inviscid medium instead define an acoustic metric.
Specified near-horizon gravitational perturbations have a fluid limit.
Their source, state and domain determine which parts can be joined.
[Hamilton--Lisle](https://arxiv.org/abs/gr-qc/0411060) supplies the river
construction, [Visser](https://arxiv.org/abs/gr-qc/9712010) the acoustic
dictionary, and [Bredberg et al.](https://arxiv.org/abs/1101.2451) the
Einstein/Navier--Stokes limit. The dictionary checks the latter's shear
coefficient directly from its perturbed Rindler metric, including the
proper-clock conversion.

One connector is the [Brown--York](https://arxiv.org/abs/gr-qc/9209012)
boundary stress. With a common spacelike normal from minus to plus,

$$
t^{\rm BY}_{ab}=\frac{K h_{ab}-K_{ab}}{8\pi G},\qquad
[t^{\rm BY}_{ab}]=S_{ab},\qquad
D_a(t^{\rm BY})^a{}_b=-T_{\mu\nu}n^\mu h^\nu{}_b.
$$

The jump is precisely Israel's material-interface law. Different material
actions can therefore occupy this mathematical interface. A horizon fluid
uses an additional limit and constitutive response on an appropriate surface;
the current pure-tension wall is one candidate in this larger map.

Douglas's circulating-scale hypothesis asks whether nested vortical regions
and transfer across scales can supply a physical continuation. Published
[holographic turbulence](https://arxiv.org/abs/1307.7267) provides a gravity/cascade
block in its own asymptotically AdS setting. The spherical river alone has no
ordinary circulation. A rotating cosmological state, its energy-transfer
budget and its light-cone observations remain calculations to construct.
The next example supplies a finite gravitational response with an explicit
material action, rather than assigning an arbitrary reflector to a horizon.

### 16.2. Two stable material interfaces in one static geometry

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-gravitational-background -->

Derivation and calculation: [background, force and response record](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-gravitational-response-v1.json).
The [derivation](../docs/nsc-atlas-gravitational-response.md) distinguishes this
candidate from the earlier formed scalar history. It reuses the
[Visser--Wiltshire](https://arxiv.org/abs/gr-qc/0310107) shell dynamics and
[Pani et al.](https://arxiv.org/abs/0909.0287) gravitational junctions.
[Jampolski--Rezzolla](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.13946) supplies the explicit
nested-gravastar antecedent; neither nesting nor gravastar perturbations are
claimed as discoveries here.

Use $G=c=M=1$ and one time normalized at infinity. The three regions have

$$
ds_i^2=-A_i^2 f_i dt^2+f_i^{-1}dr^2+r^2d\Omega^2,\qquad
f_i=1-\frac{2m_i}{r}-H_i^2r^2.
$$

The exterior has $m=1,H=0,A=1$, the parent annulus has $m=.1$, and the
central region has $m=0$. The radii are $R_o=2.5$ and $R_i=.25$.
On each surface a canonical massless scalar has homogeneous stress
$p=\sigma=s^2/2$, where s is its proper-time derivative. The bulk four-form action, with its
fixed-flux boundary term, supplies the regional vacuum stress
$\Lambda_i=4\pi G q_i^2=3H_i^2$; surface charges are the oriented flux jumps.
The material law and geometric ratios are shared declared inputs.

For ordinary increasing-radius normals, the two static Israel conditions are

$$
\sigma=\frac{\sqrt{f_-}-\sqrt{f_+}}{4\pi R},\qquad
p=\frac{1}{8\pi}\left[\frac{f'}{2\sqrt{f}}+\frac{\sqrt{f}}{R}\right].
$$

Putting $p=\sigma$ solves the interior curvature at each interface and its
matched clock. The full algebra is given in the owner; in this member,

$$
A_-^2=A_+^2\frac{f_+}{f_-},\qquad
H_{\rm parent}^2=\frac{3(29-\sqrt{265})}{1000}.
$$

| Quantity in exterior M units | Parent / outer wall | Center / inner wall |
| --- | --- | --- |
| H squared | .0381635382117 | 4.41709564871 |
| A | .541737767916 | .283041476224 |
| sigma equals p | .0120417630513 | .129330203316 |
| Radial potential second derivative | .442766777226 | 51.2125679208 |

All retained lapse factors are positive. The central bulk is nonsingular;
the two interfaces carry the specified thin material sources. Surface
conservation gives $\sigma R^4=\mathrm{constant}$. The signed wall potentials
obey $V=V'=0,V''>0$ with this law, and the normal force balance agrees
independently. These are radial linear stability results. Polar and nonlinear
stability are additional questions.

The same construction returns the conditional curvature products
$\Lambda_{\rm parent}M^2=.114490614635$ and

### 16.3. The gravitational action now composes through the interfaces

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-gravitational-composition -->

Derivation and calculation: [common-clock wave and junction controls](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-gravitational-response-v1.json).
For linear axial modes $\ell\geq2$, use the Fourier convention below
and the common-time Regge--Wheeler variable

$$
\psi(t,r)=e^{-i\omega t}\psi(r),\qquad
\psi(r)=\frac{Af h_1}{r},\qquad \frac{dx}{dr}=\frac{1}{Af},\qquad
\psi_{xx}+\left[\omega^2-U(r)\right]\psi=0,
$$

$$
U=A^2f\left(\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^2}-\frac{6m}{r^3}\right),\qquad
[\psi]=0,\quad [\psi_x]=q_s\psi,\quad
q_s=4\pi\sigma\sqrt{F_s}>0.
$$

Here $F_s=A^2 f$ is the common wall lapse squared. The shell response is
derived from Pani et al.'s general conditions $[h_0]=0$ and
$[\sqrt{f} h_1]=0$. Applying the zero-density simplification from their
special gravastar directly would give the wrong matching here. The
remaining temporal Israel condition is also checked against the canonical
surface-scalar stress. This is a coupled gravitational perturbation, not a
test field assigned a spin-two potential.
The standard Kottler bulk potential is also given by
[Cardoso--Lemos, equation 11](https://arxiv.org/html/gr-qc/0301078v2).
Their subsequent near-extremal approximation is not used here.

Let $T$ propagate $(\psi,\psi_x)$ through a region. Its determinant is 1,
and a shell adds $q_s\psi$ to the second component. Both preserve the
Wronskian flux. If $Z=\psi_x/\psi$, the regional response composes as

$$
Z_{\rm out}=\frac{T_{21}+T_{22}Z_{\rm in}}
{T_{11}+T_{12}Z_{\rm in}}.
$$

Away from a Dirichlet pole the boundary action has
$K_{11}=T_{11}/T_{12}$, $K_{12}=K_{21}=-1/T_{12}$ and
$K_{22}=T_{22}/T_{12}$. Adding adjacent actions and eliminating their shared
boundary by a Schur complement gives exactly the propagated result.
This supplies a physical gravitational realization of the atlas's composition
method in this sector.

The bulk potentials and shell strengths are nonnegative, so

$$
\int\left(|\psi_x|^2+U|\psi|^2\right)dx
+\sum_s q_s|\psi(x_s)|^2\geq0.
$$

With a regular center and radiative exterior, integration by parts excludes
finite-energy exponentially growing axial modes. This covers a propagating
gravitational sector across both material levels. It does not establish polar
stability or the nonlinear formation of a rotating hierarchy.

### 16.6. Fine motion produces an exact resolved circulation

<!-- nsc-claim:atlas-vortex-initial-transfer -->

Derivation and calculation: [initial rate, covariance and conservation checks](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-vortex-transfer-v1.json).
In cylindrical coordinates choose the smooth bump

$$
g(s)=\exp\left(1-\frac{1}{1-s^2}\right)\quad (|s|<1),\qquad
g(s)=0\quad (|s|\geq1).
$$

Set
$a(r)=g(2(r-1))$, $b=-a/2$ and $\chi(z)=g(z)$. For an integer angular
mode m and kappa=-2, the vector potential and initial velocity are

$$
\phi=m\theta+\kappa m\log r,\qquad
\mathbf A=\frac{\chi(z)\cos\phi}{m}(0,b,a),\qquad
u_0=\nabla\times\mathbf A.
$$

The field is smooth and compact, with support away from the axis, and its
divergence is zero exactly. Let U be its rotation-group mean. At the
initial instant U=0, but the covariance is

$$
R_{ij}=\langle u_{0i}u_{0j}\rangle
=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Re}(W_i\overline{W_j})
=R^{(0)}_{ij}+\frac{R^{(2)}_{ij}}{m^2}.
$$

Here $u_0=\operatorname{Re}(W e^{i\phi})$; the owner gives each component.
Both covariance terms are positive semidefinite. Their nonzero
radial--tangential correlation transfers angular momentum.

For the local classical unforced Navier--Stokes solution at any constant
positive viscosity, the initial mean viscous term vanishes because the
mean velocity is zero. Mean pressure has no tangential gradient. The
actual initial equation is therefore

$$
\left.\partial_tU_\theta\right|_0
=-\left(\partial_rR_{r\theta}+\frac{2R_{r\theta}}{r}
+\partial_zR_{z\theta}\right).
$$

The cylindrical factor two is necessary. At the symmetry plane z=0, the
envelope derivative vanishes, and substitution proves

$$
\left.\partial_tU_\theta(r,0,t)\right|_{t=0}
=-\frac{2a a'}{r^2}.
$$

For every prepared m and positive viscosity, the rates at r=.75 and 1.25 are

$$
-\frac{1024}{81}e^{-2/3}=-6.49060654184413,\qquad
\frac{1024}{225}e^{-2/3}=2.33661835506389.
$$

These are rates in reference units U squared over L. Opposite resolved
rotating bands begin to develop even though the initial mean velocity
vanishes. Direct angular averaging of the original convection term,
including its basis contribution, reproduces the stress result. This is
an initial derivative of the unforced equation, not a chosen residual force.

There is also a finite composition law. Around any admissible axisymmetric
parent B, add distinct angular modes with amplitudes $\epsilon_j$. Cross
covariances average to zero, as do the linear cross terms with B. Relative
to the solution prepared from B alone,

$$
\delta\left(\partial_t U_\theta\right)_{t=0,z=0}
=\left(\sum_j\epsilon_j^2\right)
\left(-\frac{2aa'}{r^2}\right).
$$

This composes the initial effects of prepared resolutions while retaining
their covariance state. An infinite sequence additionally needs regularity
and dissipation summability; it is not supplied by this finite identity.

### 16.13. An actual wall radar chart and a finite clock prediction

The [wall-clock construction](../results/summaries/nsc-atlas-dirac-radar-v1.json)
uses the same $H\ell=1$, $k_{\rm wall}\ell=2$, $M/\ell=.4$ and

coordinates $u_i=t_i-r_i^*$, $v_i=t_i+r_i^*$. Their wall derivatives are

$$
